### Q15 (answer)

- C1 Schwab: The 2025 10-K (period ended December 31, 2025) states total net revenues increased 22% year-over-year to $23.9 billion in 2025. [SCHW-10K-2025#130]
- C2 Schwab: The 2025 10-K states net interest revenue was $11.8 billion in 2025, up 28% from 2024, driven primarily by lower interest expense from reductions in bank supplemental funding and lower rates on funding sources, as well as growth in margin and bank lending and higher segregated cash and investments. [SCHW-10K-2025#130]
- C3 Schwab: The 2025 10-K states asset management and administration fees totaled $6.5 billion in 2025, increasing 14% from 2024, driven primarily by higher client asset balances reflecting market appreciation, asset gathering, and growth in managed investing solutions and money market funds. [SCHW-10K-2025#130]
- C4 Schwab: The 2025 10-K states trading revenue was $3.9 billion in 2025, rising 20% from 2024, driven primarily by higher trading volume. [SCHW-10K-2025#130]
- C5 Schwab: The 2025 10-K states bank deposit account fee revenue increased to $977 million in 2025, up 34% from the prior year, driven primarily by higher net yields, partially offset by lower BDA balances. [SCHW-10K-2025#130]
- C6 Schwab: The 2025 10-K states Schwab's 2025 net interest revenue growth was driven by lower interest expense from reduced bank supplemental funding and lower funding rates, growth in margin and bank lending, and higher segregated cash and investments, which more than offset lower yields on interest-earning assets. [SCHW-10K-2025#130]
- C7 Schwab: The 2025 10-K states client cash balances are a significant funding source for Schwab's revenue; cash awaiting investment may be used to extend margin loans or be swept to its banking subsidiaries, where those deposits are used to extend loans and purchase investment securities. [SCHW-10K-2025#55]
- C8 Morgan Stanley: The 2025 10-K (period ended December 31, 2025) states that within the Wealth Management segment, asset management revenues are related to advisory services associated with fee-based assets, account service and administration, and distribution of products, and are generally based on the net asset value of the account in which a client is invested. [MS-10K-2025#147]
- C9 Morgan Stanley: The fourth-quarter 2020 supplement defines advisor-led client assets as client assets in accounts that have a Wealth Management representative assigned. [MS-SUPP-2020Q4#3]
- C10 Morgan Stanley: The fourth-quarter 2020 supplement defines fee-based client assets as the amount of assets in client accounts where the basis of payment for services is a fee calculated on those assets. [MS-SUPP-2020Q4#3]
- C11 Morgan Stanley: The 2025 10-K reports an average fee rate of 78 basis points for Advisor accounts in 2025, based on asset management revenues related to advisory services associated with fee-based assets. [MS-10K-2025#177]
- C12 Morgan Stanley: In the advisor-led channel, the third-quarter 2026 supplement reports fee-based client assets of $3,155 billion at September 30, 2026. [MS-SUPP-2026Q3#16]
- C13 Morgan Stanley: In the advisor-led channel, the third-quarter 2026 supplement reports fee-based asset flows of $80.5 billion for the quarter ended September 30, 2026. [MS-SUPP-2026Q3#16]
- C14 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K (period ended December 31, 2025) states Edward Jones broadly categorizes its net revenues into four categories: fee revenue, trade revenue, net interest and dividends revenue (net of interest expense) and other revenue, net. [EDJ-10K-2025#126]
- C15 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K states fee revenue is composed of asset-based fees and account and activity fees, and asset-based fees include advisory program fees based on the average daily market value of client assets in the program, as well as contractual rates. [EDJ-10K-2025#126]
- C16 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K states asset-based fees are impacted by changes in market values of the assets and by client dollars invested in and divested from the accounts. [EDJ-10K-2025#126]
- C17 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K states account and activity fees are impacted by the number of client accounts and the variety of services provided to those accounts, among other factors. [EDJ-10K-2025#126]
- C18 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K states trade revenue is composed of commissions and principal transactions revenue; commissions are earned from the distribution of mutual fund shares and insurance products and the purchase or sale of securities, and principal transactions revenue primarily results from distribution of and participation in principal trading activities in municipal obligations, certificates of deposit and corporate obligations. [EDJ-10K-2025#126]
- C19 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K states trade revenue is impacted by the trading volume (client dollars invested), mix of the products in which clients invest and the size of trades, all of which may be impacted by market volatility, and margins earned on the transactions. [EDJ-10K-2025#126]
- C20 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K states interest and dividends revenue is earned on client margin loan account balances, cash and cash equivalents, cash and investments segregated under federal regulations, securities purchased under agreements to resell, Partnership loans and investment securities. [EDJ-10K-2025#25]
- C21 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K states service fees earned from mutual fund and insurance companies are generally based on the average daily market value of client assets held in a company's mutual fund or insurance product. [EDJ-10K-2025#208]
- C22 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K states other asset-based fee revenue consists of revenue sharing, fund adviser fees, cash solutions and Trust Co. fees. [EDJ-10K-2025#208]
- C23 Merrill: The 2025 10-K (period ended December 31, 2025) states Merrill Wealth Management's revenue of $20.7 billion in 2025 increased nine percent primarily on higher asset management fees (higher market valuations and positive AUM flows) and higher brokerage fees (increased transactional volume). [BAC-10K-2025#247]
- C24 Merrill: Trade press (September 29, 2026) reports that Merrill charges fees comparable to RIA pricing in its fee-based program, typically 1.00% and 1.40% on assets managed and as high as 1.75%. [MER-TRADE-2026-09-29-RB-01#7]
- C25 Merrill: Trade press (September 29, 2026) reports that Merrill charges full-service brokers a minimum transaction commission of $75 or $0.04 per share (whichever is greater), capped at 5.00% of principal trade value. [MER-TRADE-2026-09-29-RB-01#7]
- C26 Raymond James Financial: The 2025 10-K (fiscal year ended September 30, 2025) states the Private Client Group segment provides financial planning, investment advisory, and securities transaction services and generally charges either asset-based fees or sales commissions. [RJF-10K-2025#696]
- C27 Raymond James Financial: The 2025 10-K states the PCG segment also earns revenues for distribution and related services performed related to mutual and other funds, fixed and variable annuities, and insurance products. [RJF-10K-2025#696]
- C28 Raymond James Financial: The 2025 10-K states the PCG segment includes servicing fee revenues from third-party mutual fund and annuity companies whose products RJF distributes and from banks in the RJBDP sweep program. [RJF-10K-2025#696]
- C29 Raymond James Financial: The 2025 10-K states the PCG segment includes net interest earnings on assets segregated for regulatory purposes, margin loans, cash balances, and securities borrowing, net of interest paid on client cash balances. [RJF-10K-2025#696]
- C30 Raymond James Financial: The 2025 10-K states asset management and related administrative fees and brokerage revenues in PCG are typically correlated with the level of PCG client AUA, including fee-based accounts, and overall U.S. equity markets. [RJF-10K-2025#216]
- C31 Raymond James Financial: The 2025 10-K states that through PCG, RJF generally charges asset-based fees, presented in 'Asset management and related administrative fees', or sales commissions, presented in 'Brokerage revenues'. [RJF-10K-2025#216]

[RJF-10Q-2026Q1#299]
- C33 Raymond James Financial: The first-quarter 2026 10-Q states administrative services for PCG fee-based accounts are generally performed by the Asset Management segment, and a portion of the related revenue is shared with the Asset Management segment. [RJF-10Q-2026Q1#299]
- C34 Raymond James Financial: The first-quarter 2026 10-Q states PCG assets in fee-based accounts are a significant percentage of overall PCG AUA, and a significant portion of PCG revenues is directly impacted by market movements. [RJF-10Q-2026Q1#299]
- C35 Raymond James Financial: The first-quarter 2026 supplement states RJF earns fees from the RJBDP, a multi-bank sweep program in which clients' cash deposits in brokerage accounts are swept into interest-bearing deposit accounts at its Bank segment and third-party banks. [RJF-SUPP-2026Q1#43]
- C36 Raymond James Financial: The first-quarter 2026 supplement states RJBDP fees are included in 'Account and service fees'; PCG fees earned on deposits held by the Bank segment are eliminated in consolidation. [RJF-SUPP-2026Q1#43]
- C37 Raymond James Financial: The second-quarter 2026 10-Q (period ended June 30, 2026) states PCG earns servicing fees for administrative services related to clients' deposits swept to banks under the RJBDP; these fees vary with client cash balances, short-term interest rates, and interest paid to clients. [RJF-10Q-2026Q2#313]
- C38 Raymond James Financial: The second-quarter 2026 10-Q states that under intersegment policies, PCG receives from the Bank segment the greater of a base servicing fee or a net yield equivalent to the average yield RJF would otherwise receive from third-party banks in the RJBDP. [RJF-10Q-2026Q2#313]
- C39 Raymond James Financial: The second-quarter 2025 10-Q (period ended June 30, 2025) states PCG segment results can be impacted by changes in client cash balances and by the allocation of balances among the RJBDP, CIP, and ESP, because PCG may earn different amounts from each cash destination. [RJF-10Q-2025Q2#302]
- C40 Raymond James Financial: The June 2025 investor day deck defines asset-based revenues as including asset management and related administrative fees, asset-based brokerage revenues, net interest income, RJBDP fees, and mutual fund and annuity service fees. [RJF-DECK-2025-investor-day#91]
- C41 Raymond James Financial: On the second-quarter 2025 earnings call (period ended June 30, 2025), management stated advisors have been shifting their business to more fee-based and advisory-based relationships, and many clients have both fee-based and commission-based accounts. [RJF-TR-2025Q2#64]
- C42 Raymond James Financial: The first-quarter 2026 supplement defines domestic Private Client Group net new assets as domestic PCG client inflows, including dividends and interest, less outflows, including commissions, advisory fees, and other fees. [RJF-SUPP-2026Q1#43]
- C43 Raymond James Financial: On the first-quarter 2026 earnings call (period ended March 31, 2026), RJF's CEO stated that in fee-based accounts the average cash balance per account is less than $10,000 and that, unlike an e-broker, a financial adviser has been helping clients reinvest those cash balances; RJF is not an e-broker. [RJF-TR-2026Q1#28]
- C44 Morgan Stanley: The second-quarter 2026 10-Q (period ended June 30, 2026) states that any revenues earned by Wealth Management on client assets will vary depending upon the services and products provided. [MS-10Q-2026Q2#58]
- C45 Morgan Stanley: The third-quarter 2026 supplement defines fee-based client assets as the amount of client assets where the basis of payment for services is a fee calculated on those assets. [MS-SUPP-2026Q3#38]
- C46 Morgan Stanley: The third-quarter 2026 supplement defines self-directed client assets as active accounts which are not advisor-led, with active accounts defined as having at least $25 in assets. [MS-SUPP-2026Q3#38]
- C47 Morgan Stanley: The third-quarter 2026 supplement defines daily average revenue trades (DARTs) as the total self-directed trades in a period divided by the number of trading days during that period. [MS-SUPP-2026Q3#38]
- C48 Morgan Stanley: The 2025 10-K reports that Wealth Management asset management revenues of $18,627 million in 2025 increased 13% primarily reflecting higher fee-based assets due to higher market levels and the cumulative impact of positive fee-based flows. [MS-10K-2025#169]
- C49 Morgan Stanley: The 2025 10-K reports that Wealth Management transactional revenues of $4,588 million in 2025 increased 19% primarily driven by higher client activity across products and channels, particularly in equity-related transactions. [MS-10K-2025#169]
- C50 Schwab: Trade press (September 30, 2026) reports that wealth managers earn revenue on client cash by investing or lending uninvested balances at yields higher than the rates they pay clients, so rate increases widen the spread that drives net interest income. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#0]
- C51 Schwab: Trade press (September 30, 2026) reports that the timing and magnitude of higher-rate benefits to wealth managers turns on each firm's asset mix, funding profile, and sweep program structure. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#1]
- C52 Schwab: Trade press (September 30, 2026) reports that Fitch identified Schwab as having the largest absolute earnings exposure to client cash, reflecting its substantially larger sweep-related deposit base. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#1]
- C53 Schwab: Trade press (September 30, 2026) reports that Schwab's bank investment portfolio carries a 3.5-year duration alongside 4% floating-rate securities, meaning it would benefit from loan repricing and gradual reinvestment of fixed-rate holdings. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#1]
- C54 Schwab: Trade press (September 30, 2026) reports that Schwab generated nearly $3.4 billion in net interest revenue in Q2 2026. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#1]
- C55 Schwab: Trade press (September 30, 2026) reports that Schwab held approximately $485.7 billion in sweep accounts in Q2 2026, on which it largely generated its net interest revenue. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#1]
- C56 Schwab: Trade press (September 30, 2026) reports that Raymond James holds primarily floating-rate securities and corporate loans alongside certain variable-rate sweep fee arrangements with third-party banks, positioning it for more immediate earnings benefits when rates rise. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#2]
- C57 Schwab: Trade press (September 30, 2026) reports that LPL does not operate its own bank and instead places client deposits with partner institutions that pay it a fee, holding roughly 40% of insured cash account balances under variable-rate agreements as of Q2 2026. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#2]
- C58 Schwab: Trade press (September 30, 2026) reports that LPL generated $1.66 billion from client cash, including sweep accounts and money market accounts, in 2025. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#2]
- C59 Schwab: Trade press (September 30, 2026) reports that Ameriprise's bank investment portfolio, with a 4.2-year duration and 6% in floating-rate securities, is weighted toward fixed-rate assets, meaning repricing would be more gradual. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#2]
- C60 Schwab: Trade press (October 7, 2026) reports that net interest revenues accounted for 49.3%, or $11.8 billion, of Schwab's $23.9 billion in 2025 revenues, making cash a sensitive matter for Schwab. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-10-07-RB-01#4]
- C61 Schwab: Trade press (August 20, 2026) reports that Schwab currently makes the most revenue from managing cash, and the race is on to reduce that dependency on bank revenues. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-08-20-RB-01#3]
- C62 Schwab: Trade press (August 20, 2026) reports that industry consultant Welsh attributes Schwab's model to the cash-spread business model, saying reducing that dependency is the way to be less dependent on those assets. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-08-20-RB-01#3]
- C63 Schwab: Trade press (August 20, 2026) reports that only 5% of Schwab retail households are in fee-based advice, while CEO Wurster called the 31% who want advice a captive retail base and an unmatched conversion funnel. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-08-20-RB-01#4]
- C64 Schwab: On the second-quarter 2025 earnings call (period ended June 30, 2025), management stated clients with a financial consultant relationship bring in more than two times the net new assets and are more engaged in managed investing and banking offers than clients without a relationship. [SCHW-TR-2025Q2#11]
- C65 Merrill: Bank of America's Global Wealth & Investment Management (GWIM) foundational beliefs, as stated in the November 2025 investor day deck, include that growth of fully advised client relationships drives financial outcomes and that having the best advisors enables exceptional client outcomes. [BAC-DECK-2025-investor-day#8]
- C66 Merrill: On the second-quarter 2026 earnings call (period ended June 30, 2026), Bank of America's CFO said GWIM continued to demonstrate the strength of its advice-led, relationship-based model. [BAC-TR-2026Q2#22]
- C67 Merrill: On the second-quarter 2026 earnings call, Bank of America's CFO said continued strong growth in banking relationships and lending balances demonstrates the power of GWIM's integrated wealth and banking model. [BAC-TR-2026Q2#20]
- C68 Merrill: The 2025 10-K (period ended December 31, 2025) states GWIM reports that fees earned on AUM are calculated as a percentage of clients' AUM balances, and asset management fees charged to clients per year are commonly driven by the breadth of the client's relationship. [BAC-10K-2025#249]
- C69 The comparison is valid on this evidence as a qualitative comparison of earnings models, but it is not a same-basis numeric comparison because the firms use different revenue definitions, units, periods, and scopes. The driver of the difference is that Schwab's direct-to-investor brokerage and bank earns predominantly through the cash-spread (net interest) model on client cash, while the advisor-led models earn predominantly through asset-based fees on client assets. Schwab's 2025 10-K reports net interest revenue of $11.8 billion within $23.9 billion of total net revenues in 2025 and states client cash is a significant funding source; trade-press reporting attributes Schwab's model to the cash-spread business model, reports net interest revenues were 49.3% of Schwab's 2025 revenues, and reports only 5% of Schwab retail households are in fee-based advice. In contrast, the advisor-led firms describe asset-based fees as the core of their earnings on client assets: Morgan Stanley's 10-K states asset management revenues are generally based on the net asset value of the account; Edward Jones' 10-K states fee revenue is composed of asset-based fees based on the average daily market value of client assets plus account and activity fees; Raymond James' 10-K states the Private Client Group generally charges asset-based fees or sales commissions; and Bank of America's CFO stated GWIM is primarily a fee-based advice-driven model with about 30% of revenue as net interest income. Morgan Stanley also states that any revenues earned by Wealth Management on client assets will vary depending upon the services and products provided. [SCHW-10K-2025#130, SCHW-10K-2025#55, SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-30-IN-01#0, SCHW-TRADE-2026-10-07-RB-01#4, SCHW-TRADE-2026-08-20-RB-01#3, SCHW-TRADE-2026-08-20-RB-01#4, MS-10K-2025#147, MS-10Q-2026Q2#58, EDJ-10K-2025#126, RJF-10K-2025#696, RJF-10K-2025#216, BAC-TR-2025Q2#35]
- A1 Merrill: On the second-quarter 2025 earnings call (period ended June 30, 2025), Bank of America's CFO stated that GWIM is primarily a fee-based advice-driven model, with about 30% of its revenue now net interest income derived from GWIM clients and the large loan and deposit balances on the firm. [BAC-TR-2025Q2#35]
- A2 Merrill: On the first-quarter 2025 earnings call (period ended March 31, 2025), Bank of America's CFO stated that about 30% of GWIM revenue remains net interest income, which complements the fees earned in the advice-driven model. [BAC-TR-2025Q1#28]
- A3 Merrill: On the fourth-quarter 2024 earnings call (period ended December 31, 2024), Bank of America's CFO stated that about 30% of GWIM revenue remains net interest income, which complements the fees earned in the advice model. [BAC-TR-2024Q4#30]
- A4 Merrill: The 2025 10-K (period ended December 31, 2025) states GWIM's noninterest income primarily includes investment and brokerage services income, and in 2025 the increase was driven by higher asset management fees of $15.4 billion and higher brokerage fees from increased transactional volume, partially offset by lower AUM pricing. [BAC-10K-2025#246]
- G Edward Jones has no statement in the evidence on what drives the difference between how the direct-to-investor firm and an advisor-led model earn on client assets (the a3 cell was not found).
- G Raymond James Financial has no statement in the evidence on what drives the difference between how the direct-to-investor firm and an advisor-led model earn on client assets (the a3 cell was not found).

### Q16 (answer)

- C1 Schwab: Schwab's press release (published October 15, 2026) states it introduced Charley, a new AI-powered assistant providing U.S. retail clients with conversational access to Schwab's tools and insights. Why it might matter: Schwab was recognized as one of Fortune's 2026 Most Innovative Companies. [SCHW-PR-2026-10-15-01#3]
- C2 Schwab: Schwab's press release (published October 15, 2026) states it partnered with Anthropic to bring Claude for Financial Advisors directly to the independent RIAs it serves. Why it might matter: Morgan Stanley also describes early AI use cases, including the DevGen AI tool, Parable, and LeadIQ, an AI-powered lead distribution platform matching workplace and self-directed relationships with financial advisors. [SCHW-PR-2026-10-15-01#3, MS-TR-2025Q3#8]
- C3 Raymond James: Raymond James's investor day deck (published June 5, 2025) lists AI tools in its pipeline, including RJnet and Advisor Access GenAI Search, Zoom AI Meeting Summaries, Advisor Access Recommended Apps, GenAI Search, Microsoft CoPilot, and the NASH Platform. Why it might matter: Schwab has likewise introduced Charley, an AI-powered assistant that provides U.S. retail clients conversational access to its tools and insights. [RJF-DECK-2025-investor-day#121, SCHW-PR-2026-10-15-01#3]
- C4 Schwab: On the 3Q26 earnings call (October 2026), Schwab management said 77% of U.S. investors use AI today, while more than 90% prefer human involvement alongside AI technology. Why it might matter: Schwab says that combination of people and technology has always been central to Schwab and is a key source of its differentiation. [SCHW-TR-2026Q3#6]
- C5 Schwab: On the 3Q26 earnings call (October 2026), Schwab management said it is ready to provide access when clients want it and will move to 24/7 trading if clients want it. Why it might matter: Schwab says it has plenty of overseas clients and people working during the day who value participating outside regular market hours. [SCHW-TR-2026Q3#63]
- C6 Schwab: On the 3Q26 earnings call (October 2026), Schwab management said approximately 70% of new-to-firm retail households are Gen Z and millennials, while roughly 80% of retail assets are held by ultra-high-net-worth and high-net-worth households. Why it might matter: Schwab says it is well positioned to continue serving future generations when that wealth is passed on. [SCHW-TR-2026Q3#11]
- C7 Edward Jones: Trade press (published September 30, 2026) reports that Edward Jones has partnered with Aboon, an AI-driven third-party administrator, and Retirement Plan Advisory Group (part of Great Gray Group) to develop an end-to-end retirement plan management tool. Why it might matter: Edward Jones hopes the new technology will help its advisors better serve business owners and their employees. [EDJ-TRADE-2026-09-30-WM-01#0]
- C8 Raymond James: On the 2Q26 earnings call (July 2026), Raymond James management said AI will not replace advisers; advisers who use AI will replace advisers who do not use AI. Why it might matter: Raymond James says freeing advisers from middle-office work lets them spend more time developing personal relationships with clients and prospects, which is going to win the day. [RJF-TR-2026Q2#34]
- C9 Schwab: On the 2Q26 earnings call (July 2026), Schwab management said clients will engage in person, on the phone, on the web, on mobile, or via AI at different times in their lives and for different activities. Why it might matter: Schwab says clients value that flexibility, and that in every interaction across every channel it combines the best of people and the power of AI. [SCHW-TR-2026Q2#12]
- C10 Schwab: On the 2Q26 earnings call (July 2026), Schwab management said AI is accelerating its strategy by personalizing client experiences, enhancing productivity, and driving scalable growth. Why it might matter: Schwab says its greatest advantage comes from combining people with AI-powered capabilities to create deeper relationships that scale with clients' needs. [SCHW-TR-2026Q2#19]
- C11 Schwab: On the 2Q26 earnings call (July 2026), Schwab management said clients can access financial planning, full-service advice, tax, trust, and estate services, tax-aware strategies, banking, and lending. Why it might matter: Schwab says the RIAs it serves value its bank because they don't have to introduce another relationship to their clients. [SCHW-TR-2026Q2#11]
- C12 Bank of America: On the 2Q26 earnings call (July 2026), Bank of America management said it has over 300 AI use cases approved, of which 114 are live generative AI use cases. Why it might matter: Bank of America says these tools are designed to help its customer relationship managers prepare more thoroughly for client meetings. [BAC-TR-2026Q2#6]
- C13 Bank of America: Bank of America Private Bank (published June 17, 2026) says younger investors allocate nearly half as much to stocks as older generations, while allocating more to alternatives (15%) and crypto (13%) than older investors. - C56 Which firm claims the most about AI in advice delivery cannot be validly determined on this evidence, because the firms' claims do not share a common basis. Schwab claims it is already an AI-enabled company (1Q26, period ended March 31, 2026) and more than 220 AI use cases (4Q25, period ended December 31, 2025); Bank of America claims more than 300 AI/machine learning use cases approved, 114 live generative AI use cases, and 34 fully deployed (2Q26, period ended June 30, 2026), plus more than 200,000 teammates actively using AI-enabled capabilities and more than 400,000 prompts a day (2Q26); and Morgan Stanley claims its early AI use cases, some live and some in pilot, are showing progress (3Q25, period ended September 30, 2025). These claims cover different periods, units, scopes, and definitions, so no single shared measure supports ranking which firm claims the most. - C43 The comparison is not valid on this evidence: the question asks how preferences should shape Edward Jones's offerings, which is normative, and the documents state only what younger investors prefer — Cerulli's finding that younger investors prefer to retain a greater degree of control of at least part of their investing lives, the 2026 Bank of America Private Bank Study's figures that 65% of young investors still prefer advice from a human advisor and 87% are comfortable with advisors using AI, and Schwab's statement that younger investors need platforms, tools, education, coaching, expertise, and service — and what Edward Jones discloses (a new digital platform focused on attracting and retaining 'Next Generation investors,' and stated aspirations such as anticipating clients' needs with the right offering at the right time). The two sides are also not on the same basis: the preferences describe young investors generally, while Edward Jones's disclosures describe 'Next Generation investors' and general client segments, and no source states how preferences should shape offerings. [CERULLI-GWT-2025#1, BOFA-PB-STUDY-2026#15, SCHW-TR-2025Q3#12, EDJ-10K-2025#88, EDJ-10K-2025#8]
- C44 The evidence above only provides objective context for the strategic question asked. Whether or how Edward Jones should act is a judgment for the strategy team to make, beyond the scope of this evidence engine. ### Q24 (answer)

- C1 Morgan Stanley: Client assets per financial advisor is not computable on this evidence, because the two proposed figures cover different populations. The proposed computation cited figures from MS-SUPP-2020Q4#1. A population statement dated 2025-12-31 says "Wealth Management supports clients through three channels: Advisor-Led, Self-Directed and Workplace." (MS-10K-2025#125), and the supplement says "Advisor‐led client assets represents client assets in accounts that have a Wealth Management representative assigned." (MS-SUPP-2020Q4#3). [MS-SUPP-2020Q4#1, MS-10K-2025#125, MS-SUPP-2020Q4#3]
- C2 Edward Jones: Client assets per financial advisor is computable on this evidence for the period ended June 26, 2026, whole firm: $2,647 billion in client assets under care at period end divided by 20,514 financial advisors at period end equals $129,033,831 per financial advisor. The inputs are "Client assets under care ($ billions): Total: At period end" of $2,647 billion (EDJ-10Q-2026Q2#59) and financial advisors (actual) of 20,514 at period end (EDJ-10Q-2026Q2#60). A population statement dated 2025-12-31 describes the advisor measure as "Financial advisors (actual): At year end 20,425 20,125 19,232" (EDJ-10K-2025#130). [EDJ-10Q-2026Q2#59, EDJ-10Q-2026Q2#60, EDJ-10K-2025#130]
- C3 Raymond James Financial: Client assets per financial advisor is not computable on this evidence, because the two proposed figures cover different populations. The proposed computation cited the financial-advisor count (RJF-10K-2025#9) and client assets under administration (RJF-SUPP-2025Q3#9). The population source states "Advisors in our RCS division are not included in our financial advisor metric although their client assets are included in PCG AUA." (RJF-10K-2025#228). [RJF-10K-2025#9, RJF-SUPP-2025Q3#9, RJF-10K-2025#228]
- C4 Morgan Stanley: The January 15, 2026 strategic update deck defines Total Client Assets as "the sum of the reported Wealth Management ('WM') client assets and Investment Management ('IM') assets under management ('AuM')." The definition further states that Wealth Management client assets cover financial advisor-led brokerage, custody, administrative and investment advisory services; self-directed brokerage and investment advisory services; financial and wealth planning services; and workplace services, including stock plan administration and retirement plan services, and that certain Wealth Management client assets are invested in Investment Management products and are also included in Investment Management's AuM. [MS-DECK-2026-strategic-update#28]
- C5 Morgan Stanley: The 3Q26 supplement (published October 14, 2026) defines advisor-led client assets as "client assets in accounts that have a Wealth Management representative assigned." [MS-SUPP-2026Q3#38]
- C6 Morgan Stanley: The 3Q26 supplement cautions that "These measures are not in accordance with, or a substitute for, U.S. GAAP and may be different from or inconsistent with non-GAAP financial measures used by other companies." [MS-SUPP-2026Q3#25]
- C7 Morgan Stanley: The 3Q26 supplement defines self-directed client assets as active accounts which are not advisor-led, with active accounts defined as having at least $25 in assets; it states "Self-directed client assets represent active accounts which are not advisor-led. Active accounts are defined as having at least $25 in assets." [MS-SUPP-2026Q3#38]
- C8 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K states "The Partnership's more than 20,000 financial advisors ('FA') serve more than 9 million clients with a total of $2.5 trillion in client assets under care as of December 31, 2025." [EDJ-10K-2025#125]
- C9 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K refers to its advisors as "financial advisors" ("FA"). [EDJ-10K-2025#125]
- C10 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K reports its financial advisor count as "financial advisors (actual)" at year end and as an average for the period, shown as "Financial advisors (actual): At year end 20,425 20,125 19,232 1% Average 20,331 19,650 18,945 3%". [EDJ-10K-2025#130]
- C11 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K defines asset-based fee revenue as revenue derived from fees determined by the underlying value of client assets, including advisory programs fees, service fees, and other asset-based fee revenue; it states "Asset-based fee revenue - Revenue is derived from fees determined by the underlying value of client assets and includes advisory programs fees, service fees, and other asset-based fee revenue." [EDJ-10K-2025#207]
- C12 Edward Jones: The 2Q26 10-Q (period ended June 26, 2026) states "The Partnership ended the second quarter of 2026 with $2.6 trillion of assets under care ('AUC'), including $1.2 trillion of advisory programs AUC." [EDJ-10Q-2026Q2#61]
- C13 Raymond James Financial: The company's investor-day materials state "The definitions for client assets may be inconsistent across companies." [RJF-DECK-2025-investor-day#32]
- C14 Raymond James Financial: The 2025 10-K states "Financial advisors associated with firms in RCS are not included in our financial advisor counts, although their client assets are included in our AUA. AUA associated with firms in our RCS division totaled $217.3 billion as of September 30, 2025." [RJF-10K-2025#12]
- C15 Across the five firms: Client assets per financial advisor cannot be validly compared on this evidence. Only Edward Jones yields a computable figure ($2,647 billion in client assets under care divided by 20,514 financial advisors = $129,033,831, period ended June 26, 2026, whole firm). Morgan Stanley's and Raymond James's proposed computations were rejected because the client-asset and advisor figures cover different populations, Schwab discloses client assets but no financial-advisor headcount, and Merrill discloses no per-advisor figure. The components also are not on a common basis. Advisor headcounts differ: Morgan Stanley reports "Wealth Management representatives" (last found 15,950 at December 31, 2020); Edward Jones reports "financial advisors (actual)"; Raymond James excludes advisors in its RCS division from its financial advisor metric even though their client assets are in AUA; Schwab and Merrill disclose no comparable financial-advisor headcount (Schwab only Form ADV employee counts, Merrill only counts spanning GWIM and Consumer Banking). Client-asset measures differ: Morgan Stanley's "Total Client Assets" is the sum of Wealth Management client assets and Investment Management AuM; Schwab's "client assets" is the market value of assets in its custody, BDA balances, and proprietary products; Edward Jones reports "client assets under care"; Merrill reports "total client balances"; Raymond James reports "client assets under administration". Raymond James's investor-day materials state "The definitions for client assets may be inconsistent across companies." (RJF-DECK-2025-investor-day#32). [EDJ-10Q-2026Q2#59, EDJ-10Q-2026Q2#60, MS-SUPP-2020Q4#1, MS-10K-2025#125, MS-SUPP-2020Q4#3, RJF-10K-2025#9, RJF-SUPP-2025Q3#9, RJF-10K-2025#228, SCHW-10K-2025#118, SCHW-ADV1A-2026#1, EDJ-10K-2025#125, EDJ-10K-2025#130, BAC-SUPP-2023Q4#2, BAC-SUPP-2026Q3#53, MS-DECK-2026-strategic-update#28, MS-SUPP-2026Q3#38, RJF-10K-2025#12, RJF-DECK-2025-investor-day#32, RJF-SUPP-2026Q2#9]

### Q25 (answer)

- C1 Morgan Stanley: In its Form ADV (published November 4, 2026), Morgan Stanley states that a Morgan Stanley Virtual Advisor (MSVA) is paid a base salary plus a discretionary bonus and does not retain any portion of the Morgan Stanley Advisory Fee. [MS-ADV-2026#173]
- C2 Schwab: A trade press report (published August 20, 2026) quotes a recruiter as saying Schwab pays its W-2 advisors a salary as part of a W-2 advisor package that also includes a bonus for winning client assets, because Schwab "pays them salary and bonus for winning client assets that it largely hands them on a platter." [SCHW-TRADE-2026-08-20-RB-01#3]
- C3 Edward Jones: In its 2025 10-K (period ended December 31, 2025, published March 13, 2026), Edward Jones states that financial advisors are generally compensated on a commission basis, subject to a minimum guaranteed salary, and may be entitled to additional compensation based on their respective branch office profitability and the profitability of the Partnership. [EDJ-10K-2025#30]
- C4 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that it pays Advisors a salary and incentive compensation that is based on the revenues Merrill receives for making available and/or providing Program Services as well as for the brokerage services provided to clients with brokerage accounts. [MER-ADV-2026#402]
- C5 Merrill: On its Advisor Development Program recruiting page (page content as of November 3, 2026), Merrill lists "Base salary and unlimited earning potential" among what the firm offers advisors. [MER-PR-web-advisor-development-program#1]
- C6 Raymond James Financial: In its Form ADV (published September 14, 2026), Raymond James states that newly affiliated RJA financial advisors through the Advisor Mastery Program may receive a salary in addition to advisory fees (or salary in addition to commissions and trails in the case of transactions in brokerage accounts) for a period of time. [RJF-ADV-2026#50]
- C7 Raymond James Financial: In its Form ADV (published September 14, 2026), Raymond James states that the salary paid by RJA declines each year as the number of clients the financial advisor serves and receives revenues in the Advisor Mastery Program increases. [RJF-ADV-2026#51]
- C8 Morgan Stanley: In its 2025 10-K (Item 7, period ended December 31, 2025), Morgan Stanley states that for certain revenue-producing employees in the Wealth Management and Investment Management business segments, compensation is largely paid on the basis of formulaic payouts that link employee compensation to revenues. [MS-10K-2025#153]
- C9 Morgan Stanley: In its Form ADV (published November 4, 2026), Morgan Stanley states that the amount of the advisory fee a client pays is a factor used in calculating the compensation paid to the client's Financial Advisor, and that if the fee rate is below a certain threshold, the Financial Advisor is given credit for less than the total amount of the fee. [MS-ADV-2026#173]
- C10 Morgan Stanley: A trade press report (published October 26, 2026) reports that Morgan Stanley is increasing a pay threshold for advisors by 10% in 2027, meaning advisors have to generate that much more revenue to see the same pay next year. [MS-TRADE-2026-10-26-IN-01#4]
- C11 Edward Jones: In its Form ADV (published March 27, 2026), Edward Jones discloses that a portion of brokerage sales charges or commissions is paid to the financial advisor, that a financial advisor will typically earn more in upfront fees and commissions when the client uses brokerage services, and that a financial advisor will typically earn more over time if the client invests in Advisory Solutions Fund Models. [EDJ-ADV-2026#65]
- C12 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that an Advisor is credited in the form of "production credits" with a portion of the Merrill Lynch Fee paid and, for brokerage accounts, the Sales Charges received from securities transactions. [MER-ADV-2026#402]
- C13 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that Advisors also receive production credits based on clients' use of margin lending, in respect of cash swept to Bank Affiliates, and for referrals of clients to BANA and other Merrill Affiliates for banking, lending and other financial services, including trade execution. [MER-ADV-2026#402]
- C14 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that Advisors receive "production credits" toward their compensation formula based on the amount of the Merrill Lynch Fee that the client pays for their Account. [MER-ADV-2026#403]
- C15 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that Advisors receive fewer production credits (by 10% or more) if the weighted average Merrill Lynch Fee Rates for a client's Accounts and for the Accounts that are part of the Household are lower than the rate levels for the specific wealth asset tiers Merrill establishes. [MER-ADV-2026#403]
- C16 Raymond James Financial: On its affiliation options page (page content as of April 21, 2026), Raymond James discloses that the Traditional Employee affiliation model offers up to 50% payout plus a full array of competitive employee benefits offerings. [RJF-PR-web-affiliation-options#1]
- C17 Raymond James Financial: On its affiliation options page (page content as of April 21, 2026), Raymond James discloses that the Advisor Select affiliation model offers a 70-80% gross payout plus a full array of competitive employee benefits offerings. [RJF-PR-web-affiliation-options#1]
- C18 Raymond James Financial: On its affiliation options page (page content as of April 21, 2026), Raymond James discloses that the Independent Contractor affiliation model offers an 81% to 90% payout. [RJF-PR-web-affiliation-options#1]
- C19 Raymond James Financial: On its affiliation options page (page content as of April 21, 2026), Raymond James discloses that the Corporate RIA affiliation model offers an 81% to 90% payout. [RJF-PR-web-affiliation-options#2]
- C20 Raymond James Financial: In its Form ADV (published September 14, 2026), Raymond James states that in his or her capacity as a registered representative, a financial advisor earns commissions for recommending transaction-related services in a brokerage account and may receive commissions and/or trail fees for brokerage transaction services. [RJF-ADV-2026#85]
- C21 Raymond James Financial: In its Form ADV (published September 14, 2026), Raymond James states that depending on a financial advisor's annual revenue generation with the Firm, the financial advisor can receive a higher portion of the Fee paid to the Firm (therefore, the Firm retains less of the Fee paid). [RJF-ADV-2026#50]
- C22 Raymond James Financial: On its earnings call for the period ended March 31, 2026 (published April 22, 2026), Raymond James stated that even on the employee side it has tiered payout systems, so as production increases advisors get higher up on the payout grid. [RJF-TR-2026Q1#44]
- C23 Raymond James Financial: On its earnings call for the period ended March 31, 2026 (published April 22, 2026), Raymond James stated that independent advisor payouts are higher than employee advisor payouts because independents cover their own overhead costs such as real estate and health insurance. [RJF-TR-2026Q1#44]
- C24 Edward Jones: In its Form ADV (published March 27, 2026), Edward Jones discloses that the Program Fee, as well as assets under care and Reserve Line Advance balances, will impact most financial advisors' eligibility for a bonus and the bonus amount. [EDJ-ADV-2026#67]
- C25 Edward Jones: In its Form ADV (published March 27, 2026), Edward Jones discloses that most financial advisors are eligible to participate in the Edward Jones Travel Award Program, which includes domestic and international travel, or a cash award in lieu of a trip. [EDJ-ADV-2026#67]
- C26 Edward Jones: In its 2025 10-K (Item 1A), Edward Jones states that if the Partnership's profitability decreases, then bonuses paid to financial advisors may be decreased or eliminated. [EDJ-10K-2025#58]
- C27 Edward Jones: In its 2025 10-K (Item 1A), Edward Jones states that a significant portion of its more successful financial advisors' compensation is variable based on the Partnership's profitability. [EDJ-10K-2025#58]
- C28 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that Advisors are eligible to receive a compensation award, payable over a defined period of time, from an incentive program that is currently based on meeting growth targets from the prior year in new households and in assets and liabilities. [MER-ADV-2026#406]
- C29 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that the growth in assets and liabilities component of the award is based on the movement of client assets into accounts enrolled in the Program and other investment advisory programs, and on investment activity in specified investment products. [MER-ADV-2026#406]
- C30 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that an Advisor is subject to a reduction in their incentive compensation grid if they fail to achieve growth for two consecutive years in the assets and liabilities component of the award. [MER-ADV-2026#406]
- C31 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that for Program Accounts, Advisors receive increased compensation based on achieving strategic objectives, including the growth in their clients' participation in banking services and Lending Programs offered by Merrill, BANA and other Bank Affiliates. [MER-ADV-2026#443]
- C32 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that Advisors do not receive additional compensation as a result of advisory client assets held in the Cash Sweep Program. [MER-ADV-2026#406]
- C33 Raymond James Financial: In its Form ADV (published September 14, 2026), Raymond James states that financial incentives for initial/ongoing affiliation with the Firm include bonuses, among other arrangements such as transition assistance, deferred compensation arrangements, enhanced pay-outs, repayable business transition or working capital loans, and administrative fee reimbursements. [RJF-ADV-2026#54]
- C34 Raymond James Financial: In its Form ADV (published September 14, 2026), Raymond James states that qualification for recognition clubs can result in benefits to the financial advisor such as paid travel, reimbursement of certain business-related expenses, and deferred compensation awards. [RJF-ADV-2026#53]
- C35 Raymond James Financial: On its earnings call for the period ended June 30, 2025 (published July 23, 2025), Raymond James stated that it will not have quotas, push products, or create variable incentives to try to drive growth in products. [RJF-TR-2025Q2#59]
- C36 Morgan Stanley: In its 2025 10-K (Item 7), Morgan Stanley states that deferred compensation for financial advisors in the Wealth Management business segment is generally composed of 75% cash-based awards and 25% stock-based awards. [MS-10K-2025#193]
- C37 Morgan Stanley: In its 2025 10-K (Item 7), Morgan Stanley states that its deferred cash-based compensation programs and stock-based compensation programs for current and former employees, including financial advisors in the Wealth Management business segment, generally contain vesting, clawback and cancellation provisions. [MS-10K-2025#193]
- C38 Morgan Stanley: In its first-quarter 2026 10-Q (period ended March 31, 2026), Morgan Stanley states that employees are permitted to allocate the value of their deferred cash-based awards among a menu of notional investments selected by the Firm, including fixed income, equity, commodity and money market funds. [MS-10Q-2026Q1#73]
- C39 Morgan Stanley: In its first-quarter 2026 10-Q (period ended March 31, 2026), Morgan Stanley states that in the first quarter of 2026 the Firm reduced the amount of deferred compensation as a proportion of total compensation for Wealth Management representatives (advisors). [MS-10Q-2026Q1#74]
- C40 Morgan Stanley: In its 2025 10-K (Item 8), Morgan Stanley states that certain current and former employees of the Firm, including financial advisors in the Wealth Management segment, participate in the Firm's stock-based compensation plans, which include RSUs, PSUs and an ESPP. [MS-10K-2025#781]
- C41 Edward Jones: In its Form ADV (published March 27, 2026), Edward Jones discloses that assets under care and client Reserve Line Advance balances may also impact a financial advisor's eligibility for the receipt of certain limited partnership profits interest in The Jones Financial Companies, L.L.L.P. (the "Profits Interest"). [EDJ-ADV-2026#67]
- C42 Raymond James Financial: In its 2025 10-K (Item 8), Raymond James states that its RSU award activity includes grants to employees, independent contractor financial advisors, and members of the Board of Directors, for the year ended September 30, 2025. [RJF-10K-2025#678]
- C43 Raymond James Financial: In its second-quarter 2026 10-Q (period ended June 30, 2026), Raymond James states that it has one share-based compensation plan, the Amended and Restated 2012 Stock Incentive Plan, for its employees, Board of Directors, and independent contractor financial advisors. [RJF-10Q-2026Q2#210]
- C44 Raymond James Financial: On its traditional employee advisor web page (page content as of May 16, 2026), Raymond James states that as an employee advisor, the advisor owns their book of business. [RJF-PR-web-traditional-employee-advisor#0]
- C45 Morgan Stanley: A trade press report (published October 26, 2026) reports that Morgan Stanley is not introducing retention bonuses as part of its 2027 pay plans for advisors. [MS-TRADE-2026-10-26-IN-01#4]
- C46 Morgan Stanley: A trade press report (published November 10, 2026) states that a FINRA arbitration award shows Morgan Stanley sought to claw back $2.46 million in recruiting bonus loans from an advisor, and the notes at issue were paid in full before the hearing. [MS-TRADE-2026-11-10-IN-01#0]
- C47 Morgan Stanley: A trade press report (published August 28, 2026) states that Morgan Stanley sued a former advisor in a dispute over promissory notes (loans that brokers pay back to firms over time) and sought to claw back recruiting bonus money, and the arbitrator awarded Morgan Stanley close to $8.7 million. [MS-TRADE-2026-08-28-IN-01#0]
- C48 Schwab: A trade press report (published September 23, 2026) states that Schwab and other RIA custodians typically do not recruit advisors with bonus money. [SCHW-TRADE-2026-09-23-RB-01#2]
- C49 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that advisors who join Merrill from other firms generally receive payments in connection with that move, which may take various forms including salary guarantees, upfront awards and various forms of compensation contingent on continued employment such as deferred payments and recruitment loans. [MER-ADV-2026#408]
- C50 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that the amount paid to Advisors under these recruitment arrangements is generally based to a large extent on the size of the business at their prior firm. [MER-ADV-2026#408]
- C51 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that future recruiting payments based on assets that transfer are conditioned on the total assets in accounts that they service at Merrill, and these bonuses are in addition to the incentive compensation to which they are otherwise entitled as Advisors. [MER-ADV-2026#408]
- C52 Raymond James Financial: In its fiscal 2026 second-quarter supplement (period ended June 30, 2026), Raymond James defines PCG recruiting and retention-related compensation as including expenses related to cash and equity awards issued in conjunction with recruiting activities, as retention for existing advisors, or in conjunction with acquisitions, with such awards expensed over the requisite service period (typically between 5 and 10 years). [RJF-SUPP-2026Q2#45]
- C53 Raymond James Financial: In its fiscal 2026 second-quarter supplement (period ended June 30, 2026), Raymond James reports Private Client Group recruiting and retention-related compensation of $117 million for the three months ended June 30, 2026, compared with $97 million for the three months ended June 30, 2025. [RJF-SUPP-2026Q2#14]
- C54 Raymond James Financial: In its fiscal 2026 second-quarter supplement (period ended June 30, 2026), Raymond James reports Private Client Group recruiting and retention-related compensation of $335 million for the nine months ended June 30, 2026, compared with $274 million for the nine months ended June 30, 2025. [RJF-SUPP-2026Q2#14]
- C55 Raymond James Financial: On its earnings call for the period ended September 30, 2025 (published October 22, 2025), Raymond James stated that in fiscal year 2025, adjusted compensation expense included the amortization of transition assistance provided to recruited advisors and other retention awards to existing advisors in the aggregate amount of $355 million, an increase of approximately 11% compared to fiscal 2024. [RJF-TR-2025Q3#20]
- C56 Raymond James Financial: In its first-quarter 2026 10-Q (period ended March 31, 2026), Raymond James states that it offers loans to prospective financial advisors for recruiting and retention purposes and had unfunded commitments of $19 million for loans to financial advisors who have met such conditions as of March 31, 2026. [RJF-10Q-2026Q1#161]
- C57 Raymond James Financial: On its earnings call for the period ended December 31, 2025 (published January 28, 2026), Raymond James stated that 100% of what it pays in transition assistance goes to retention of the advisor, not to a seller. [RJF-TR-2025Q4#44]
- A1 Schwab: In its 2026 investor day deck (published May 14, 2026), Schwab states that it uses adjusted diluted EPS and return on tangible common equity as components of performance criteria for employee bonus and certain executive management incentive compensation arrangements. [SCHW-DECK-2026-investor-day#289]
- A2 Schwab: In its 2025 10-K (Item 8), Schwab states that capitalized contract costs relate to incremental costs of obtaining a contract with a customer, including sales commissions paid to employees for obtaining contracts with clients. [SCHW-10K-2025#406]
- A3 Schwab: In its 2025 10-K (Item 15), Schwab's exhibit index lists a Form of Notice and Stock Option Agreement for Non-Employee Directors under The Charles Schwab Corporation Directors' Deferred Compensation Plan II and successor plans. [SCHW-10K-2025#570]
- A4 Schwab: In its 2025 10-K (Item 15), Schwab's exhibit index lists a Form of Notice and Restricted Stock Unit Agreement for Non-Employee Directors under The Charles Schwab Corporation Directors' Deferred Compensation Plan II and successor plans. [SCHW-10K-2025#570]
- A5 Edward Jones: In its 2025 10-K (Item 11), Edward Jones discloses a qualified deferred compensation plan (profit sharing and 401(k) plan) in which each named executive officer participates and which also covers all eligible general partners and service partners of the Partnership and associates of the Partnership's subsidiaries. [EDJ-10K-2025#306]
- A6 Edward Jones: In its second-quarter 2026 10-Q (period ended June 26, 2026), Edward Jones discloses the existence of a non-qualified deferred compensation plan, with mutual fund securities held to economically hedge future liabilities for the plan. [EDJ-10Q-2026Q2#40]
- A7 Schwab: In its 2025 10-K (Item 8), Schwab states that the fair value of its restricted stock units that vested during 2025 was $295 million. [SCHW-10K-2025#493]
- A8 Schwab: In its 2025 10-K (Item 8), Schwab states that the fair value of its restricted stock units that vested during 2024 was $281 million. [SCHW-10K-2025#493]
- A9 Schwab: In its 2025 10-K (Item 8), Schwab states that the fair value of its restricted stock units that vested during 2023 was $288 million. [SCHW-10K-2025#493]
- A10 Schwab: In its 2025 10-K (Item 1), Schwab states that its employee compensation package includes equity awards in addition to base pay, performance-based incentive pay, and recognition awards. [SCHW-10K-2025#50]
- A11 Merrill: In its Form ADV (published October 30, 2026), Merrill states that advisors receive compensation in the form of production credits based on a percentage of the loan revenue on margin loans and Affiliate Lending Program loans, and receive greater compensation the more the client borrows or the higher the interest rate. [MER-ADV-2026#490]
- A12 Merrill: A trade publication (published September 29, 2026) reports that when BlackRock acquired Merrill Lynch Investment Managers in 2006, Merrill got a 49% equity stake in BlackRock, and Bank of America later sold its remaining stake in 2011. [MER-TRADE-2026-09-29-RB-01#4]

[EDJ-10K-2025#70]

### Q26 (answer)

- C1 Raymond James Financial: Trade press reported (2026-11-12) that Raymond James will add $18.7 billion in client assets to its Financial Institutions Division, the unit dedicated to bank and credit union advisors, as an existing FID program merges with Synovus Financial Corp. Why it might matter: the source states the deal expands Pinnacle Financial Partners' relationship with Raymond James. [RJF-TRADE-2026-11-12-WM-01#0]
- C2 Raymond James Financial: Trade press reported (2026-11-12) that Raymond James will add $18.7 billion and 70 advisors to its Financial Institutions Division as an existing FID program merges with Synovus Financial Corp., with advisors expected to transition in May 2027. Why it might matter: the source states the deal expands Pinnacle Financial Partners' relationship with Raymond James. [RJF-TRADE-2026-11-12-WM-01#0]
- C3 Raymond James Financial: Trade press reported (2026-11-06) that Raymond James & Associates welcomed a three-advisor team from PNC Private Bank that managed over $1.5 billion in client assets, operating as KKM Wealth Partners within Alex. Brown in Washington, D.C. Why it might matter: the source states the practice focuses on comprehensive wealth management for high-net-worth individuals, multigenerational families, corporate executives, foundations. [RJF-TRADE-2026-11-06-IN-01#0]

### Q28 (answer)

- C1 Edward Jones: The 3Q25 10-Q (period ended September 26, 2025) reported financial advisor attrition of 6.1% for the third quarter of 2025, defining "Attrition %" as "the annualized number of financial advisors that left or retired from the Partnership during the period compared to the total number of financial advisors as of period end." The 10-Q states: "The Partnership ended the third quarter with 20,429 financial advisors. Financial advisor attrition was 6.1% as a result of a variety of factors, including a rise in financial advisor departures with tenure of five years or less." [EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#70, EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#69]
- C2 Edward Jones: The 2Q25 10-Q (period ended June 27, 2025) reported that financial advisor attrition increased to 6.4% in the second quarter of 2025, "primarily due to an increase in retirements, consistent with industry trends." The 10-Q states: "The Partnership ended the second quarter with 20,309 financial advisors. Financial advisor attrition increased to 6.4% primarily due to an increase in retirements, consistent with industry trends." [EDJ-10Q-2025Q2#67, EDJ-10Q-2025Q2#66]
- C3 Edward Jones: The 3Q25 10-Q (period ended September 26, 2025) reported financial advisor attrition of 4.7% for the third quarter of 2024, in a table row reading "Attrition %(5) 6.1% 4.7% 30% 6.0% 5.1% 18%". [EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#68, EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#69]
- C4 Edward Jones: The 3Q25 10-Q (period ended September 26, 2025) reported financial advisor attrition of 6.0% for the nine months ended September 26, 2025, in a table row reading "Attrition %(5) 6.1% 4.7% 30% 6.0% 5.1% 18%". [EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#68, EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#69]
- C5 Edward Jones: The 3Q25 10-Q (period ended September 26, 2025) reported financial advisor attrition of 5.1% for the nine months ended September 27, 2024, in a table row reading "Attrition %(5) 6.1% 4.7% 30% 6.0% 5.1% 18%". [EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#68, EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#69]
- C6 Merrill: On the 2Q26 Bank of America earnings call (transcript published July 14, 2026), CFO Alastair Borthwick said Merrill's "adviser attrition is now at near historic lows. So we're pretty happy with that." The passage states no numeric attrition rate. [BAC-TR-2026Q2#63]
- C7 Edward Jones: The 2025 10-K (period ended December 31, 2025, published March 13, 2026) states: "The Partnership experiences continued efforts by competing firms to hire away its financial advisors, although the Partnership believes its rate of attrition of financial advisors is in line with comparable firms." [EDJ-10K-2025#27]
- C8 A numeric comparison of the two firms' financial advisor attrition rates, or of either rate against an industry attrition rate, is not valid on this evidence. Edward Jones discloses an annualized attrition rate — 6.1% for the third quarter of 2025 and 6.4% for the second quarter of 2025, defined as "the annualized number of financial advisors that left or retired from the Partnership during the period compared to the total number of financial advisors as of period end" — but Merrill discloses no attrition rate number; the only Merrill item is Bank of America CFO Alastair Borthwick's 2Q26 statement that "adviser attrition is now at near historic lows." The industry research figures are not an attrition rate either: they measure advisors' self-reported likelihood of leaving their employer within the next 12 months (Struggling advisors 4X as likely as Thriving; nearly 1 in 10 Struggling extremely likely; 1 in 50 Thriving extremely likely). Edward Jones itself offers only a qualitative comparison, stating in its 2025 10-K that the Partnership "believes its rate of attrition of financial advisors is in line with comparable firms," and in its 2Q25 10-Q that the 6.4% increase was "consistent with industry trends." [EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#70, EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#69, EDJ-10Q-2025Q2#67, EDJ-10Q-2025Q2#66, BAC-TR-2026Q2#63, KITCES-WELLBEING-2023#17, EDJ-10K-2025#27]
- A1 Industry research: A research report (published December 20, 2023) found that "Compared to Thriving advisors, those Struggling were 4X as likely to be leaving their employer within the next 12 months." [KITCES-WELLBEING-2023#17]
- A2 Industry research: The same research report (published December 20, 2023) found that "Nearly one in 10 Struggling advisors were extremely likely to be leaving their employer compared to just 1 in 50 Thriving advisors." [KITCES-WELLBEING-2023#17]
- A3 Industry research: The same research report (published December 20, 2023) found that just 1 in 50 Thriving advisors (wellbeing rating of 9 or 10) were extremely likely to be leaving their employer. [KITCES-WELLBEING-2023#17]

### Q29 (answer)

- C1 Morgan Stanley's 2025 Form 10-K (period ended December 31, 2025, Item 7) states: "For certain revenue-producing employees in the Wealth Management and Investment Management business segments, compensation is largely paid on the basis of formulaic payouts that link employee compensation to revenues." [MS-10K-2025#153]
- C2 On the 4Q 2025 earnings call (published January 15, 2026), management said that in the first quarter of 2026, in its words, "we will also increase the cash component of our advisor compensation. We are making these changes to reduce the accounting-driven volatility in revenues and earnings," supporting its overall investment into its financial advisors and simplifying its compensation program. [MS-TR-2025Q4#22]
- C3 Morgan Stanley's 2Q 2026 Form 10-Q (period ended June 30, 2026, Item 2) states: "Additionally, in the first quarter of 2026, the Firm reduced the amount of deferred compensation as a proportion of total compensation for Wealth Management advisors." [MS-10Q-2026Q2#94]
- C4 Trade press reports (published October 26, 2026) that "As it has in the past, Morgan Stanley is increasing a pay threshold for advisors by 10% in 2027, meaning advisors have to generate that much more revenue to see the same pay next year." [MS-TRADE-2026-10-26-IN-01#4]
- C5 Trade press reports (published October 26, 2026) that Morgan Stanley for years has taken a small amount of an advisor's gross revenue, called it deferred compensation, and required advisors to stay for years in order to vest. [MS-TRADE-2026-10-26-IN-01#1]
- C6 Trade press reports (published October 26, 2026) that "Morgan Stanley and Merrill Lynch are not introducing retention bonuses as part of their 2027 pay plans for advisors, adhering to a more traditional approach." [MS-TRADE-2026-10-26-IN-01#4]
